 Legislation of Copyright
The main directions of the state policy in the field of copyright and other related rights are
as follows:
① Creation of works of science, literature and art, stimulation of activit ies aimed at
enhancing the moral values of the people;
② Creation of a legislative framework that defines and protects copyright s in
accordance with advanced international practices;
③ Organization of public administration in the field of copyright and related rights;
④ Development of international relations with a view to p rotecting the rights of
authors in the territories of other states.

The minimum amount of the author's fee shall be determined by the relevant executive
authority. The development and integration of Azerbaijan’s national patent system to the
international level, and the strengthening of international cooperation in this field, are
prioritized by the state policy. As a member of the World Intellectual Property Organization,

===== PDF_PAGE 90 =====
2022 K-Innovation Partnership Program with Azerbaijan
78
Azerbaijan actively cooperates with EU countries in the field of IP rights , and has signed
intergovernmental agreements and MoUs for bilateral agreements with EU countries.

 Intellectual Property Network
The role of IP rights is fostering innovations and tech commercialization. However, the idea
that a stronger IP system leads to greater innovation and creativity could benefit from more
data and a quantitative analysis.
In Azerbaijan, businesses are often unaware that their IP is valuable business asset that
could formulate their competitive advantage in the marketplace. Therefore, the first thing
that one should do is to protect one’s business IP in a country where one is currently doing,
or is planning to do, business in the future.
The Intellectual Property Agency of Azerbaijan is responsible for formulating a unified state
policy in the field of IP rights and ensuring its implementation, as well as guaranteeing legal
protection of IP rights, in order to prevent their appropriation and the violation of rights related
to their use, and to take appropriate measures in this regard. It is also responsible for organizing
the protection and management of IP rights in digital networks to support the development of
the information society, including digital information technologies and electronic commerce,
and for taking measures to support the formulation of IP policy in scientific research institutions,
and commercialization of the results of intellectual activities and innovative technologies related
to the creation of infrastructure for commercialization and technology transfer.

 Status of Patent Experts
The term IP encompasses key fields such as copyright, patents, and trademarks. The links
between patents and innovation s depend on the related industry and the influenced
markets. Importantly, patents have a critical impact on market growth and could either
foster or hinder innovation and tech commercialization, which should be taken into
consideration when developing and reorganizing the National IP System of Azerbaijan.
IP experts are key actors of the National IP System and include lawyers, scholars, and
policymakers. Specifically, a patent attorney is a specialist who is qualified to advise the
market on patents and other intellectual p roperty rights, and who assists with the
acquisition of patents granted by patent offices around the world. Cooperation with
individuals, universities, start-up companies and large corporations is one of the most
essential activities of a patent attorney in adapting to the different market needs of the
country.
Moreover, there are various IP Law Firms in Azerbaijan staffed with professional patent
attorneys and experts who can provide the full range of IP protection services in the
Azerbaijan market to diverse companies ranging from small businesses to large corporations.

===== PDF_PAGE 96 =====
2022 K-Innovation Partnership Program with Azerbaijan
84

Experts Affiliation and Roles

attorney
[lawyer]

On the instructions of the principal, a patent attorney prepares, issues, obtains,
maintains and performs other legally significant works related to inventions, utility
models, industrial designs, trademarks and geographical indications;
A patent attorney represents the power of attorney in the implementation of rights
to inventions, utility models, industrial designs, trademarks and geographical
indications, as well as in the issuance of rights and the conclusion of license
agreements;
A patent attorney represents the Authorized Property Agency of the Republic of
Azerbaijan and its subordinate organizations, courts, and other bodies as a plaintiff
or defendant within its powers; and advises the proxy in the field of protection of
industrial property.
The Patent attorney has the following rights:
• to receive remuneration from the principal while serving him/her;
• to request documents from departments and organizations on his/her behalf in
connection with the performance of his/her powers within the scope of his/her
powers, and to submit these documents or their copies to the principal;
• to establish a patent service enterprise in accordance with the established rules
or to engage in professional activities without a legal entity.

3. Start-up Policy and Programs

 Business Conditions and the Direction of Economic Policy in
Azerbaijan
Based on the World Bank's “Doing Business” ranking, from an external perspective,
Azerbaijan's business environment and start-up ecosystem continue to rise in the rankings
and have been evaluated as “Good”.
Azerbaijan announced ‘Azerbaijan 2030: National Priorities for Socio -Economic
Development’, which presents the country’s vision for economic development over the next
ten years, emphasizing the development of the non-oil sector and manufacturing industry,
and private-led growth to promote it. In order to respond to the new strategic 'P ost-
COVID19' stage of development etc , the following four priority tasks related to the
environment and green growth have been proposed: create a competitive economy that
continues to grow; establish a dynamic and inclusive society based on social justice; foster
innovative and competitive human capital; and promote return to resettlement in the
restored territories. In particular, the non -oil sector should be placed at the center of
economic growth in terms of discovering new engines for sustainable growth, deepening
economic diversification and export-oriented industrialization, and transforming Azerbaijan
into an economy that creates high added value through innovative a nd creative private
initiatives.
As regards the national start-up fund, there is a million dollar question for Azerbaijan ,
namely, “Which industries should be prioritized for fostering and promoting at the national
level?” From a research perspective, it is arguable that the best way would be to pick the
winners based on their industry rather than providing a “level playing field” for all start-ups
in the country. In reality, however, hardware start-ups face far greater challenges compared
to the software and service industries. For example, fintech is growing slowly, but there are
few start-ups (e.g. BankOff, Payriff, OnePoint, etc.). Start-ups in fintech usually offer loyalty
programs, financial aggregators, etc. E-commerce is also one of the most attractive fields

===== PDF_PAGE 98 =====
2022 K-Innovation Partnership Program with Azerbaijan
86
for certain start-ups (e.g. Nextsale, 11delivery, etc.). The challenge facing e-commerce and
marketplaces in Azerbaijan largely originates from the weak local manufacturing industries,
compared to larger economies. Turkey’s organized its first “Teknofest” event in Azerbaijan
in 2022 as the Teknofest 2022: Aviation, Space and Technology Festival. Considering the
success of the Turkish defence industry, the same founding team’s interest in Azerbaijan
could also foster defence-tech industry start-ups in the near future.
From a financial perspective, it is difficult for start-ups to obtain or attract game-changing
investments. Therefore, incubation and acceleration centers usually work with industry-
leading companies to organize joint programs so that their start-ups can obtain funding
from companies at the end of their j ourneys. For example, INNOLAND organized such a
program with Azergold, a local gold-mining agency, in a bid to find start-ups in mining,
geology, and similar fields. However, such programs usually have a limited budget and do
not distribute the actual amount of funds raised to the winning start-ups.
Regarding the total number of start-ups in Azerbaijan, it is important to note that there is
not a single database which encompasses all the statistics about all start-up companies and
their staff. Small and Medium Business Development Agency (KOBIA), under the Ministry of
Economy, grants start-up certificates and lists them on their webpage. So far, forty-three
start-up companies have been granted such certificates. In reality, however, this figure is
selective and does not cover the whole picture. As such, the relevant authorities plan to
collect such statistics in the recent future. The main obstacles facing start-ups in Azerbaijan
can be listed as follows:
[Table 3-9] Difficulties Facing Start-ups in Azerbaijan
Factor Limitations
Culture
• Lack of family encourages and supports the development of
entrepreneurship (a large number of approaches hinder entrepreneurial
behavior).
• Low need for innovation.
• Insufficient integration of the country’s population into the information
dynamics of the world.

===== PDF_PAGE 99 =====
Chapter 3. Current Status of the Technology Commercialization System and Programs in Azerbaijan
87
Factor Limitations
• the safer jobs and social benefits of the public sector still play a significant
role in taking risks among youth (the unwillingness of youth take risks).
Human
Resources
• Lack of young people with sufficient knowledge, skills and abilities.
• Weak approach to teamwork.
• Little emphasis on specialist management.
Technology
• Weaknesses of strategic approaches to the use of potential resources,
which have a scientific basis, and unwillingness to follow global technology
trends.
Market
• Small domestic market.
• Poor development of value-added sectors.
• Lack of attention to long-term strategic approaches in big business.
• Lack of environment to support the development of export potential
products.
Finance
• Preference for traditional methods in the investment environment.
• Weaker domestic financial markets in which start-ups and innovation
projects can aim to get listed
• Lack of funds and legal basis (no venture fund until 2022).
Environment
• Lack of comprehensive incentives and supportive legislation.
• Poor development of business partnerships.
• Preference for non-professional approaches to business management.
• Low interest in mass production among manufacturing enterprises.
• Low interest in deep business development.
Source: Bunyamin Seyidov and Isa Gasimov (2022) “Current Status of Azerbaijan”

In addition, according to KOTRA’s 2018 report on overseas market research, start-up and
incubator programs are being operated by the government, but in many cases they are still
operated as exhibitions due to the poor business environment. It was found that very few
start-ups are discovered through this program. The local circumstances that hinder the
development of start-ups are as follows:
① (Closed economy) Due to Azerbaijan’s close geographical proximity to Russia and
Turkey and its small market, foreign companies show little desire to enter the
market. It has a closed economic structure in which capital liquidity is insufficient
in the private sector and capital is concentrated in only a few companies.

===== PDF_PAGE 100 =====
2022 K-Innovation Partnership Program with Azerbaijan
88
② (Talent outflow) As the vested interests are very dominant and there is no
background in a society centered on blood ties and delays, it is difficult to be
incorporated into the vested interests, so many excellent talents head overseas.
③ (Conglomerate-oriented economy) Since high -ranking public officials implicitly
tolerate company ownership, large corporations owned by high -ranking officials
have greater dominance in the industry, making it difficult for new start-ups to
emerge.
④ Tax burden) Tax audits of general private companies are frequent and taxation is
high. In many cases, companies are run by voice.
⑤ (Education system) The overall quality of education is low due to the low salaries
of faculty members and poor educational facilities.

As a result, while the transition to a sustainable growth structure in terms of the industry
and export structure is still considered insignificant, the diversification of manufactured
goods and the rapid increase in exports of non-petroleum products are positive in terms of
economic diversification.

 Azerbaijan’s SEM support policy
Azerbaijan launched the National Fund for Entrepreneurship Support in 1992 as an agency
affiliated with the Ministry of Economy, Trade and Industry. Its core function is to provide
necessary information or technology to founders, and its main goal is to provide financial
support for start-ups at low interest rates. (The interest rate is 6%, which is very low
compared to the market interest rate of 17-18%) The fund is mainly invested in greenhouse
complexes, agricultural product distribution centers, grain storage and processing facilities,
and livestock product distribution centers. Zero percent is invested in the agricultural sector.
The fund amounted to approximately US $1.5 billion from its establishment until 2014, but
it is now known to amount to approximately $350 million per year.
Azerbaijan's industry has developed with the main focus on the oil and gas industry, as befits
the country’s nickname, 'Land of Fire'. As of 2019, the oil and gas industry accounted for

===== PDF_PAGE 101 =====
Chapter 3. Current Status of the Technology Commercialization System and Programs in Azerbaijan
89
about 38% of GDP and 90% of exports. However, due to the drop in oil prices since 2015,
Azerbaijan’s currency, the Manat, has been devalued more than twice, and the limitations
of the resource-dependent economic structure have been sharply felt. As such, various
policy changes were attempted with an emphasis on fostering start-ups and small and
medium-sized enterprises (SMEs).
In this regard, the Azerbaijani government announced the “Strategic Road Maps for the
National Economy and the Main Economic Sectors” in December 2016, which are focused
on the development of the non-oil industry and the fostering of small and medium-sized
enterprises (SMEs). The main aim is to strengthen the country’s capacity for economic
diversification, including the downstream development of the oil and gas industry from a
simple resource export, the development of the agricultural, medical, pharmaceutical, and
tourism industries, and the fostering of small and medium-sized enterprises (SMEs).
Among them, the Agency for Small and Medium Business Development of Azerbaijan was
established in December 2017 as a measure for fostering small and medium -sized
enterprises, and along with the ASAN Center. Azerbaijan has also established a business
center for small and medium-sized enterprises (SMEs) to provide education, consulting, and
financial and administrative services to small and medium-sized enterprises (SMEs).
In March 2018, financial support for start-ups was provided through the Youth Fund, while
the tax law was amended to exempt companies from income tax for three years (i.e.
companies that receive a Start-up Certificate).
In August 2018, the Entrepreneurship Development Fund, established under the Ministry of
Economy, became independent , and a budget of 1.1 billion manat (about US$ 645
million/KRW 807.6 billion) was allocated to private companies in the non-oil industry. This
fund is being used in a variety of ways, from educating entrepreneurs to providing financial
support for promoted projects. The industries receiving the most by order of priority are the
agriculture, tourism, information and communication, and manufacturing industries. In
addition, the Ministry of Transport and Information and Communication supports the
holding of a start-up contest jointly with the Heydar Aliyev Foundation.

 Azerbaijan's start-up programs and potential by sector
The majority of start-up and incubator support programs are led by the government, and
the representative programs of the following institutions are currently being executed.
Moreover, there are newly emerging innovation centers and units within corporations, such
as Pasha Holding and the International Bank of Azerbaijan OJSC. Such corporations organize
and support incubation programs and attract start-ups mainly, but not only, for their own
corporate needs. Their innovation programs can be categorized as follows:
• Intrapreneurship by which they attract ideas internally;
• Entrepreneurship by which they attract ideas from outside the organization.

However, these corporate sector players have not actively engaged in technology
commercialization or made any significant attempts to do so.
① Start-up Azerbaijan: An organization established by the Ministry of Transport and
Information and Communication to promote start-ups and strengthen links with new
start-ups. www.start-up.az
② Azerbaijan Start-up Ecosystem: A site that collects comprehensive information about
the start-up ecology for young entrepreneurs. www.start-upazerbaijan.az
③ Next Step Innovation Center (www.icnextstep.com): An organization that provides
funds to the founders of start-ups who have completed the education provided by
the organization ranging from product concept to prototype development. With an
office in Baku City, office space is provided to selected prospective founders.
④ Social Innovation Lab SIL (Social Innovation Lab SIL): A research institute established
to nurture fifty technology start-ups every year until 2025, providing founder
education and overseas market entry services. www.socialinnovationlab.az
⑤ SUP (www.sup.az): An intensive training program for nurturing start-ups. It provides
a 3-month education course on product invention and marketing practices.
⑥ Support for start -up programs of local telecommunications companies. Barama
(www.barama.az): Start-up support project of Azercell Telecom, a telecom operator,
Bakcell AppLab (www.applab.bakcell.com): A program by Bakcell, a telecom operator,
to support start-up founders' mobile app development.

 Start-up Activities in Azerbaijan
3.4.1 New Ideas Contest 39
3.4.1.1 What is the New Ideas Contest?
The New Ideas Contest is a project organized by the former Qafqaz University Techno park
(QU Techno park). Originally established at QU Techno park in 2013, the contest is currently
held once a year in the Techno park of Baku Engineering University to support the
development of ecosystems and the development of innovative entrepreneurship. At the
same time, it aims to encourage entrepreneurship among young people and support and
assist them with the development of their start-up projects. The New Idea LLC acts as a real
bridge between the world of work and enterprising young people, as well as investors. The
competition is open not only to members of the Baku Engineering University, but also to
anyone living in the Republic of Azerbaijan.

39 http://yenifikir.az/

93
3.4.1.2 Mission Objectives
• To support innovative entrepreneurship.
• To prepare the groundwork for the formation of an ecosystem in the Techno park,
which is important for the development of high technologies.
• To support the sustainable socio-economic development of the country.
• To support the development of ICT.
• To assist young entrepreneurs in setting up their own companies.
• To develop an entrepreneurial approach.

3.4.1.3 Purpose
The purpose of this competition is to strengthen the innovation economy by reinforcing the
ecosystem in the BMU Techno park and contributing to the emergence and application of
innovative ideas in the field of technology. Its main targets are as follows: Start-ups, mobile
applications, internet, software, games, software as a service, corporate software, new
business models, services, robotics, information technology and consumer electronics, and
health (health) information technology, etc.

3.4.1.4 Target
• Formation of internationally competitive companies that can play a role in the
development of the country and the region.
• Development of high technologies.
• Development of entrepreneurial approaches and innovation.
• Incorporation of business ideas that can turn a product into money.
• Support for the development of intellectual properties.

3.4.1.5 Forms of support for project development
• Initial financial support (at least 40 start-ups received grants of approx. 1,000 to

prizes, was about 150,000 AZN).

• Support for finding seed investment . (The investment fund of the New Idea s

• Study of international experience of companies with a promising business idea.
• International market integration.
• Media and lobbying support.

3.4.1.6 Who is eligible to participate in the competition?
Those who want to build a business with a good business idea.

3.4.1.7 Evaluation Criteria
• Innovation and technological innovation: What are the technological ly innovative
features, efficiency, new and technological advantages of an idea or project?
• Market strategy and competitiveness: Buyers of a product or service, reasons for
buying, characteristics of the target market, market size and development potential,
competitors and market valuation approaches.
• Teamwork and management: The skills and experience of the project team, the role
of each member in the project, any staff shortcomings related to the project and
how to overcome them.
• Presentation quality: Preparation and skills required to present a project (or
business idea) in a short time (3-7 minutes).
• Business plan: Specificity, work opportunities and chronology, work methodology,
financial planning, reliability of analysis and forecasts based on research results.
• Supported projects.

four times so far. The development of twenty-nine of these projects was supported by the

3.4.1.8 What is the Start-up Azerbaijan Competition?
The main goal of the Start-up Azerbaijan project is to improve the investment climate in the
country’s ICT sector , identify the best Internet and IT projects, and help transform
innovative ideas into successful businesses. An innovative idea is an activity aimed at
presenting a product or service through the effective use of information technology. The
ultimate goal of the submitted projects should be to build a real business, provide services,
including social services, and attract customers. It is possible to propose projects related to
any existing business ideas, in which case the proposed project should be based on more
effective innovations. Projects should not be of the research type. Such projects are not
accepted for competitions and other contests held within the "Start-up Azerbaijan" initiative.
Business projects can be submitted by a team of one or more people. Projects that can be
used both globally and locally can be submitted to the competition. Expert evaluations of
start-ups participating in the competitions is organized within the framework of "Start-up
Azerbaijan". Start-up Azerbaijan is not just a competition but rather a project in which
training is arranged for start-ups and meetings are held with investors. Start-ups are helped
to form a team. For those who want to start an Internet business, training is held within the
framework of the project in order to provide opportunities to gain knowledge and
experience of starting and running a successful business.
The Start-up Azerbaijan team assists selected start-ups in presenting successful projects to
investors and in finding investors, as well as providing investors with access to attractive
projects. It also organizes s tart-up meetings and discussions to achieve the set goals.
Although Start-up Azerbaijan presents symbolic prizes to the winners, the projects are

===== PDF_PAGE 108 =====
2022 K-Innovation Partnership Program with Azerbaijan
96
directly funded by presentation s to investors and various funds. Start-up Azerbaijan
provides financial and support for successful internet projects, meetings with investors, and
internships with the support of local and foreign experts. Start-up Azerbaijan aims to help
those who want to create a new Internet business to realize their dreams, attract
investment, and manage their business.

3.4.1.9 Evaluation criteria
• How attractive is the project financially and realistically?
• How well does the team know the market and business features?
• How clear and understandable is the business plan or review?
• Can the proposed structure-project be a market leader?
• Was the team able to create a prototype of the project?
• How successful and experienced is the team?
• Do one or more team members have the skills needed to promote ideas or services?
• Does the team have experience of working with investments or any specific plans
in this regard?
• Approximately how long can a project survive in the market?

entrepreneurial skills among young people.

• Support projects in the ICT sector.

3.4.2.3 Purpose
The main goal of the Barama Incubation Center, which has achieved many successes
during its six years of operation, is to increase the number of interesting projects by
strengthening the development of the start-up ecosystem.

1. Technology Commercialization Ecosystem

 Mid-Long Term Scenario
Technology commercialization is the process of connecting technologies, products or
services, and markets. In other terms , it is a process by which various obstacles are
overcome in order to introduce the immature technologies produced by a laboratory into
the market.
Therefore, the commercialization of a technology basically requires technologies, financial
resources, and human resources, but also needs to be harmonized specific models must be
designed differently according to a given environment (e.g. governance, institutions,
consensus, etc.).
From this perspective, two scenarios are conceivable based on the existing technology
commercialization environment in Azerbaijan: One is the main platform model for national-
level integrated support by pan-ministries, and the other is the specialized organization
model dedicated to technology commercialization by the HTP under the Ministry of Science
& Education.

1.1.1 Scenario A: AZ InnoCom (tentative name)
AZ InnoCom is the main control tower of, and serves as the central axis for, national
technology commercialization in Azerbaijan. In reality, it is a pan-ministerial promotion
committee for technology commercialization, and thus forms part of the current ‘state
companies and state services’ (including the State Migration Service, State Border Service,

Chapter 4.
Policy Recommendations

===== PDF_PAGE 120 =====

State Security Service, Foreign Intelligence Service, State Mobilization and Conscription

1.1.1.1 Current Governance for Technology Commercialization
Azerbaijan is already striving to create a favorable environment for innovation. For example,
it has ministries that support innovation and provide governmental R&D support, as well as
various related agencies (e.g. IDDA) and innovation clusters (e.g. Innoland), etc.
Furthermore, on April 6, 2021, the President of the Republic of Azerbaijan signed the Decree
on the “Establishment of a Center for Commercialization and Technology Transfer" under
the Intellectual Property Agency of the Republic of Azerbaijan. Thus, the Center has been
established as a legal entity of public law whose remit is to establish communication,
promote mutual order and coordinate proposals between science and industry ; and
promote technology transfer to ensure the qualitative growth of inventive and innovative
activities. In addition, its adoption of an applied nature and commercialization of the results
of intellectual activity serve to support innovative development, including start-up projects
aimed at transferring the results of scientific research for use for commercial and non -
commercial purposes, as well as generating income from these results, providing clients
with services for the su pply of technologies and projects to their target markets, and
coordinating activities in this area (from a report drawn up by Azerbaijan local experts). The
HTP was established to transfer and commercialize ANAS’s technology, while universities
such as ADA, UNEC, etc. have set up organizations dedicated to technology
commercialization.

1.1.1.2 Governance reorganization for Technology Commercialization
The basic pillars of the policy are based on the relevant laws, as follows:
• (Law) Technology Commercialization Act or Innovation Act . The competent
ministries of this law are economic and industrial -related ministries, such as the
Ministry of Economy and the Ministry of Digital Development and Communications
of the Republic of Azerbaijan.
• (Control Tower) Policy governance is structured around the control tower created
by pan-governmental linkage. The control tower, a decision-making body, is a joint
committee of the relevant ministries, named AZ InnoCom. The basic law will specify
the establishment of the committee. The membership of the committee is
composed of ministers from each depar tment. Its m embers design national
strategies and set the roles, tasks and budgets of each ministry for the next five
years, and each ministry in turn establishes its annual action plans and projects.
• (Plan) The committee sets the government’s mid -term strategy for technology
commercialization and adjusts its direction every five years. An expert group is
established to develop an appropriate plan. Each ministry recommends two experts
– including foreign experts - with a wealth of experience in such fields as markets,
enterprises, research, investment, brokerage, and incubation (scale-up,
demonstration, etc.). In addition, an internal and external innovation trend research
team has been established as a permanent planning body. Each ministry checks the
implementation of its plan every two years , and submits a report on its progress
and performance to AZ InnoCom, which in turn reviews and revises the plan if
necessary.

1.1.2 Scenario B: ‘AZ Innovate’ (tentative name)
AZInnovate has been established as a more extensive and specialized organization under
Azerbaijan’s Ministry of Science and Education than ANAS-HTP in order to support start-ups
as well as technology transfer. Therefore, the functions and roles of the existing ANAS-HTP
as an affiliated organization of the ANAS will be further strengthened as an agency under
the Ministry. AZInnovate basically needs four departments, namely, IP management,
Technology Incubation, Enterprise, and Cooperation.

1.1.2.1 IP Management Department
The IP Management Department manages the IP life cycle and designs policies for the
planning, protection and dissemination of IP created in laboratories. For example, there is

===== PDF_PAGE 126 =====
2022 K-Innovation Partnership Program with Azerbaijan
114
an IP R&D system that investigates the IP required by a target product and reflects it in R&D
planning, thus enabling the necessary IP development to be performed. This process also
makes it possible to improve patent analysis techniques or expertise. In order to operate
such a system, however, it is necessary to be able to access the patent information network
and global databases such as PatSnap, Espacenet, and Patentscope, and, above all else, it is
essential to accumulate patent information internally. Furthermore, information on the
R&D output of universities and research institutes should be integrated into one site. To
that end, a country -level data platform, i.e. the NTB(National Technology Bank 45 ), is
required. The obligation to register R&D output (patents) information should be specified in
the provisions of the Basic Law for Technology Commercialization. In this way, the national
patent capability can be checked, and technology consumers can search for required
technologies. In addition, such information can be used in R&D planning.

1.1.2.2 Technology Incubation Department
First, the department needs seed finding, which is the process of finding technologies that
can be used for technology transfer or start-ups based on an analysis of accumulated
technical information, i.e. the NTB. It is also the process of finding technologies that can
overcome the technological limitations of SMEs.
It bundles technologies required for target products or services and plans additional R&D
for missing parts. Additional R&D is carried out through industry -university-PRO (Public
Research Organization) collaboration, and the final technology is transferred to the
company concerned. For this kind of R&D, the R&D budget is funded by the government or
by corporate matching. It evaluates the technology value for calculating prices and royalties
required for technology trade and licensing contracts. Valuation is approached via an
objective evaluation system and/or subjective expert judgment. In Korea, public institutions
have developed and used evaluation models such as SMART (KIPO), TTRS (KIBO), and Star-
value (KISTI). Banks and private companies also have their own valuation systems. Generally,
it is necessary to design a guideline for a technology transfer contract so that a standard

contract form can be used by universities, PROs and companies. In Korea, the ‘Guidelines

1.1.2.3 Enterprise Department
It is important to create a venture ecosystem for innovative growth in which scale-up
technology can be used as a seed for start-ups. The Enterprise Department offers support
for start-ups, such as providing space, matching initial capital, connecting technology and
experts, and providing regulatory improvement, tax incentives. In particular, when the
domestic market is small, support for overseas expansion and public procurement is
important. It is also important to train market experts, accelerators, investors, company
builders, coordinators, and tax accountants. In particular, cooperation with VCs, the main
players in the venture ecosystem, is also important. Therefore, AZInnovate should form the
Azerbaijan VC Association and support its activities, and share NTB and start-up information
with it. This process will serve as an opportunity for VCs to be more active in the venture
ecosystem. Technology and enterprise incubation should be linked w ith the national
innovation infrastructure (Innoland, Incubation centers, Techno park, etc.), the legal basis
for which should be specified in the Basic Law for Technology Commercialization.

1.1.2.4 Cooperation Department
The Cooperative Department has various roles and functions related domestic and
overseas matters.
First, its domestic programs include the Help Desk, public relations, Tech Fair and trend
briefs among other things. Help Desk is a corporate counseling center that serves as a
window for companies to receive consultation on technical difficulties or needs. PR
includes activities designed to convey the importance of technology commercialization
to the public, researchers, and enterprises, etc. Tech Fair is a large-scale annual event at
which people involved in technology commercialization gather and promote their
achievements and prototypes and new products are exhibited. There are also awards
for best practices.
Global cooperation on approaches to forming the value chain necessary for technology
commercialization is also underway , including a supply chain approach and a market

perspective approach. The department also prepares briefs on various global trends related
to technology commercialization and presents them to governments, universities, research
institutes, investors (VCs), and companies.

organizations. In order to achieve better outcomes, cooperation with related entities is
needed, but not as an independent project. In particular, it is important to establish

relations between universities, research institutes, and HTP are required throughout the
entire commercialization cycle.

increase the number of the organization’s personnel and provide training to enhance their
expertise.

products can be tested, check and designed. Professional technicians and prototype
managers are offered residencies through the TiR (Technicians in Residence) system, which
is designed to support budding entrepreneurs.

2. Intellectual Property Management Policies

 Introduction
This chapter proposes a number of options which the Azerbaijan government may consider
in fine-tuning its IP management policies for technology transfer and the commercialization
of home-grown technologies and intellectual properties (i.e. patents) which cover such
technologies.
To this end, this chapter focuses on three main topics which are prerequisite to efficient
technology transfer and the commercialization of such technologies and patents. These
topics include the following:
(1) Building an invention -friendly IP ecosystem by enhancing patent awareness
among potential inventors ( e.g. professors, graduate students, and researchers
[either scientists or engineers ] in Azerbaijan) as well as among government
employees;
(2) Determining whether or not to enact (or amend) laws or regulations regarding:
(a) ownership of inventions and patents arising from R&D sponsored by the
government of Azerbaijan; and
(b) ownership of inventions and patents conceived by the employees of universities,
research institutes or private companies (i.e. “employee inventions”);
(3) Determining whether or not to establish an organization (i.e. a technology
licensing office or technology transfer office) within universities and research
institutes, where such an organization would be responsible for:
(a) acquiring inventions conceived by inventors working for those universities and
research institutes;
(b) determining whether or not to seek patent protection for such inventions (e.g.
filing patent applications, prosecuting those patent applications, paying
maintenance fees, and such like);
(c) determining whether or not to pursue patent protection abroad;
(d) determining (if yes for c above) in which countries a patent should be obtained
and maintained;

===== PDF_PAGE 133 =====
Chapter 4. Policy Recommendations
121
(e) transferring technology to manufacturers or service providers; and
(f) motivating more inventors to conceive more valuable inventions.
Each of the following sections relates to each of the three topics mentioned above. It
is taken as understood throughout this chapter that:
(1) the subject of technology transfer is patents granted to Azerbaijan-grown
technologies;
(2) technologies covered by such patents and developed by:
(a) professors, students (either graduate or undergraduate), and other engineers and
researchers of Azerbaijan universities;
(b) researchers (either scientists or engineers) and other employees in Azerbaijan
research institutes; and
(c) researchers (either scientists or engineers) and other employees in Azerbaijan
companies.
(3) the patented technologies which are covered by the patents are developed
through R&D sponsored by the government of Azerbaijan;
(4) the providers (or owners) of such technology-based patents are:
(a) Azerbaijani universities;
(b) Azerbaijani research institutes; and
(c) Azerbaijani companies.
(5) the recipients ( e.g., the licensees) of such patents are manufacturing companies
(or service providers), either domestic or international.

 Building an Invention-Friendly IP Ecosystem
The successful transfer of a technology and its subsequent commercialization typically
require many valuable patents, where new and competitive technologies (or inventions)
must precede such valuable patents. However, it is almost impossible to obtain such
valuable patents, unless the inventors or innovators come up with new and competitive
technologies (or inventions).
This i mmediately raises the following questions: How many new and competitive
technologies do you need for successful technology transfer? How many c ommercially
valuable patents does one need for a “bonanza” commercialization?

===== PDF_PAGE 134 =====
2022 K-Innovation Partnership Program with Azerbaijan
122
In fact, one would need many new, innovative, and competitive technologies as well as
numerous commercially valuable patents for successful technology transfer and bonanza
commercialization. Why is this? Because our common sense gives us such a hint. Because
the layman’s statistics says so. In addition, because the Pareto principle states so. And more
importantly, because those who have done that say so.
This section aims to provide the background to a virtuous IP ecosystem in which many
competent inventors create lots of high-quality technologies (or inventions), in which many
competent legal agents produce valuable patents from high-quality inventions, and in which
numerous professionals commercialize such patents for profit.
Based on this background, this section introduces various projects that have been
conducted by a Korean government agency (i.e. the Korea Invention Promotion Association
[KIPA]), and derives from them a few policies which the Azerbaijan government may
benchmark in building an invention-friendly IP ecosystem on Azerbaijan’s soil.

2.2.1 Why We Need Lots (and Lots) of New Innovative Technologies and
High-Quality Patents
The process of commercializing a technological innovation is a complicated process. To
achieve the successful commercialization of a given new technology, that technology must
generally possess (1) technical viability, (2) marketability, and (3) patentability, to name just
a few requirements.
Many Technologies Fail due to the Complexity of the Technology Commercialization
Process
More importantly, technical viability, marketability, and patentability do not work in a
parallel mode but rather in a serial mode. That is, successful commercialization requires that
all three requirements must be met.
Therefore, even when a new technology satisfies two of the three abovementioned
requirements, the attempt to commercialize it will fail if it does not satisfy any one of them.

===== PDF_PAGE 135 =====
Chapter 4. Policy Recommendations
123
(A) Technical Viability of a New Technology
First, a new technology must possess a feature that is superior to many competing, existent
technologies. A new technology should also remain competitive, at least for the period of
its commercialization.
Regardless of the superiority of a new technology, the licensee, e.g. the manufacturer,
usually seeks to prove its viability. Therefore, when the licensee-manufacturer acquires a
technology from a university etc. through licensing, the manufacturer must prove the
viability of that technology by, for example, making a prototype and testing it, performing
bench experiments, and so on.
Once a technology’s technological viability has been proved, the manufacturer may proceed
to scale up the bench process to a full-scale process for mass production. To that end, the
licensee may have to invest heavily in buying land, building the factory and testing its
facilities, and such like.
If a manufacturer fails in any of the above steps, the commercialization strategy will fail. For
example, embodiment of the technology may prove to be too expensive, the facility for
mass production may be too complicated, its reproducibility may be inconsistent, and so on.
In other words, regardless of the superiority of a new technology, there is no guarantee that
it will be commercially viable.

(B) Marketability of Products that Embody a New Technology
Even if a manufacturer comes up with an innovative scale-up process and succeeds in mass
producing the related products based on a new and innovative technology through a viable
mass-production process, the products must sell in the market.
As we know from past experience, many products that were technically superior to existing
competing products nevertheless failed in the market for various reasons. One pertinent

===== PDF_PAGE 136 =====
2022 K-Innovation Partnership Program with Azerbaijan
124
example of this is what commentators refer to as a “vitamin or pain-killer” problem47,48 or as
a “vitamin, pain-killer, or cure” problem.49 Another example may be a product that is based
on a solid, new, and innovative technology but which happens to have a less attractive
design, or deliver under-par performance, and such like.
Whatever the actual problems may be, a product which does not sell in the market is
regarded as a failure of commercialization. The refore, the marketability of a new and
superior technology may be at least as important as its technical superiority.

(C) Patentability of a New Technology and Protection of the Patent
Even in cases where a new technology is superior to competing technologies and has been
successfully scaled up for mass production, and where the related products may already
have succeeded in gaining a sizable market share, its commercialization may still fail if there
is rampant copying of the products by copiers.
This is when the patent comes in to protect the manufacturer from copiers by allowing the
manufacturer to exercise exclusive rights against the copiers and to protect its market share.
However, the exclusive rights come from the written disclosure and the claims of the patent
which covers a new technology and which also covers any novel and non-obvious features
of the mass-producing processes. Accordingly, when a patent is not properly drafted, the
manufacturer cannot fend off such copying competitors. In the worst case scenario, the
manufacturer’s patent could be invalidated, leaving it defenseless against the copies of
competitors.
Furthermore, even when a manufacturer’s patent is properly written, the legal system
within which the manufacturer operates may not be willing to enforce the patent to its full
strength. As a result, the court may tolerate rampant copying of the manufacturer’s

47 George Deeb, Forbes (2014), ｢Is your start-up building a ‘vitamin’ or a ‘painkiller’?｣.
48 Hackernoon (2020), ｢Painkillers, vitamins and misunderstood start-up lessons｣.
49 O’Reilly Media, Inc. (2013), ｢Vitamin, painkiller, or cure?｣, Ch. 2, ｢The agile start-up: quick and dirty lessons every
entrepreneur should know｣.

===== PDF_PAGE 137 =====
Chapter 4. Policy Recommendations
125
innovative technology, and the manufacturer may not be able to defend its market from
competitors who sell their counterfeit copies at a ridiculously low price.

(D) Human Factors
Last but not the least in importance is human error. That is, regardless of the technical
superiority and viability of a new technology, its huge marketability, and strong patentability,
there is still a strong chance that the CEO of the manufacturer may make one or more fatal
mistakes, which could result in the total failure of the commercialization of that technology.
As briefly discussed above, successful technology commercialization presupposes that a
new technology and the patent covering it satisfy three key requirements. These
requirements must be met one after another in a series mode. The managerial board of the
manufacturer must also make reasonable and timely decisions. Considering all of the above,
it naturally follows that any attempt to commercialize a new and innovative technology is
more likely to fail than to succeed.

Successful Technology Commercialization Is Exceedingly Rare
Whether it is pure common sense or is based on hard science, the Pareto principle50 states
that for many outcomes, roughly 80% of the consequences come from 20% of the causes
(the “vital few”). The Pareto principle is also known as the “80/20 rule”, the “law of the vital
few” or the “principle of factor sparsity” to cite but a few of its other names. It then follows
from this principle that about 80% of attempts to commercialize a new and innovative
technology are doomed to fail from the outset, while only about 20% of such attempts
succeed to some extent.
However, the Pareto principle actually seems to overestimate the chances of success of
technology commercialization. In fact, many empirical studies indicate that the chances of
successfully commercializing a new and ground-breaking technology to a potential licensee

50 Wikipedia (2022), “Pareto principle”.

===== PDF_PAGE 138 =====
2022 K-Innovation Partnership Program with Azerbaijan
126
are pretty slim.
One report s uggests that “globally less than 5%” of the technologies developed in
universities “make it to an actual commercial product that generates revenue for both the
inventors and the university.”51
In addition, although Stanford University has received about 10,000 new inventions over
the past forty-five years, “just three had generated multimillion-dollar licenses by 2016.”
While recognizing that technology commercialization is a complex series of processes and
that most attempts to commercialize new, innovative technologies fail miserably, it should
be noted that there is not only a statistical but also a simple and reasonable solution to
achieving successful technology commercialization:
① Create as many innovative inventions as possible.
② Obtain as many high-quality patents as possible.
③ Contact as many potential licensees (e.g. Manufacturers) as possible.
④ Transfer as many technologies along with the related patents as possible.
⑤ Confirm whether the licensee manufacturer pays the correct amount of royalties
on a certain day.

Of course, the strategy exemplified above may seem to be somewhat akin to a food recipe
that does not specify which ingredients should be included in what amounts.
However, it still remains true that, all other things being equal, a society which generates
more technologies is far more likely to have successful cases of technology
commercialization than a society which generates fewer technologies.
In this context, the following section presents a few strategies which the government can
adopt (1) to create more technologies, inventions, and patents on a macroscopic scale, and
(2) to establish a virtuous IP ecosystem.

51 Stephen Rodda, Financial Review (2021), “Commercializing new technologies needs a two-way partnership”.

===== PDF_PAGE 139 =====
Chapter 4. Policy Recommendations
127
Establishing an Invention-Rich Ecosystem to Generate Lots of Inventions
Innovative inventions and new technologies do not come from nowhere. That is to say, such
inventions and technologies are neither created from scratch nor obtained at no cost.
Rather, inventions and technology come from an ecosystem comprising several layers.
It has been noted that different people who are equipped with different skill sets usually play
a major role in each such layer. In addition, these people tend to work sequentially so that, for
instance, people in the second layer cannot start to perform their task until the people in the
first layer have completed their task, while people in the third layer cannot start to perform
their tasks until people in the second layer have finished their work, and so on.
To reflect these characteristics, one can set up a hypothetical intellectual property (IP)
ecosystem, and then use the IP ecosystem in analyzing the characteristics of each layer as
well as various interactions among the different layers.
[Figure 4-7] Graphical Presentation of an IP Ecosystem or an IP-Pyramid

Source: Y. Shim (2016), “The IP Silk Road”

[Figure 4-7] is a graphical representation of an exemplary intellectual property ecosystem,
where such an ecosystem is referred to as an “IP-Pyramid.” As illustrated in [Figure 4-7], the

===== PDF_PAGE 140 =====
2022 K-Innovation Partnership Program with Azerbaijan
128
ecosystem includes an upstream, a midstream, and a downstream , each of which is
connected to the others in a series mode.
(A) Upstream of the IP Ecosystem
The upstream of the IP ecosystem is characterized by the conception of an invention by an
inventor. More particularly, the upstream starts when an inventor is struck with an idea
about an innovative technology. The inventor then modifies his or her idea and ends up with
plenty of related ideas. Eventually, the inventor sublimates his best idea into an invention
(i.e. the inventor conceives the invention). Accordingly, there is no doubt that the inventor
or the innovator is the main character in the upstream layer.

(B) Midstream of the IP Ecosystem
The midstream of the IP ecosystem is characterized by the filing of a patent application and
the issuance of a patent. More particularly, the inventor hires a patent lawyer (or a patent
agent), who then prepares and files a patent application to a patent office in the country (or
another country) of the inventor.
After filing the application, the patent lawyer deals with a patent examiner at the patent
office, and assists his client in getting the patent application issued as a patent. Therefore,
the patent lawyer or the patent agent is the main character in the midstream.

(C) Downstream of the IP Ecosystem
Then, the downstream of the IP ecosystem is characterized by different modalities of
commercializing (or monetizing) the patent. More particularly, the inventor (or patent
owner) can resort to at least three different modalities to reap benefits from his invention.

===== PDF_PAGE 141 =====
Chapter 4. Policy Recommendations
129
(C-1) The 1st Modality of Commercialization = Self-Practicing the Patent
The first modality of commercialization is that the inventor applies his invention and
makes his own product. To this end, the inventor may have to set up a start -up.
However, it would be even better if the inventor himself were the owner of his own
factory.
In this first modality of commercialization, the inventor creates sales revenue by selling
his product in the market, and then takes a portion of the sales revenue as his profit.
Of course, the commercialization fails when the inventor cannot sell his product in the
market or when the balance stays in the red even after the inventor has generated
sales revenues.

(C-2) The 2nd Modality of Commercialization = Technology (and Patent) Transfer
The second modality for commercialization is that the inventor transfers his
technology and patent to a licensee for profit. Scientific or technological innovators
are usually not entrepreneurs. As such, they like to work in the lab, not on the
manufacturing front. Accordingly, most of these innovators tend to be looking
constantly for a potential licensee-manufacturer. As the inventor finds and negotiates
with a licensee -manufacturer and signs a licensing contract, the inventor can earn
royalties in various timings , i.e. (1) an upfront royalty, (2) a running royalty, (3) a
milestone royalty, and so forth.
The upfront royalty corresponds to the royalty which an inventor receives upon signing
a contract for technology transfer. Conversely, the running royalty corresponds to a
portion of the licensee’s revenue. That is, when the licensee generates sales revenue,
the licensee shares a certain portion of the revenue with the inventor, and that portion
corresponds to the running royalty. The milestone royalty is the one which the licensee
pays to the inventor when a certain condition is met or when a certain event happens.
It should be noted that the various types of royalty discussed above reflect different
features of technology transfer and technology commercialization.
For example, a technology transfer that includes the upfront royalty , but not the
running royalty , means that the real fruit of technology commercialization is kept

===== PDF_PAGE 142 =====
2022 K-Innovation Partnership Program with Azerbaijan
130
solely by the licensee. The refore, once the contract for technology transfer has been
signed, there will be very little, or no, incentive for the inventor to assist the licensee
in carrying out the actual commercialization.
Conversely, a technology transfer that includes the upfront royalty as well as the
running royalty means that the inventor and the licensee agree to share the real fruits
of the technology commercialization.
Accordingly, compared with the upfront-only licensing outlined in the above paragraph,
this type of t echnology transfer can provide plenty of incentives for an inventor to
assist the licensee in carrying out and then successfully completing the actual
commercialization.

(C-3) The 3rd Modality of Commercialization = Asserting the Patent
Some circumstances may force an inventor to go to the court to sue a competitor for
its infringement of his or her patent . In one instance, an inventor self-practiced his
patent, manufactured the products, and sold them in the market. However, a
competitor made a copy-cat product, and sold the counterfeit product in the market.
Due to the lack of (or lax) patent protection in the country, the inventor lost his market.
Although the inventor had proposed to license the patent, the copying competitor
refused. In this case, the inventor’s only remaining option was to take the case to court,
sue the copying competitor , win the lawsuit, and obtain damages from the copying
manufacturer. In some instances, the inventor may not be interested in self-practicing
his own patent at all. Instead, the inventor meets lots of manufac turers, advertise his
new technology and patent, and propose a licensing deal with those manufacturers.
However, the manufacturer chooses not to sign the contract, but to copy the patented
technology of the inventor. Similar to the above example, the inventor’s only
remaining option is to take the case to court, sue the copying competit or, win the
lawsuit, and then obtain damages from the copying manufacturer.

(D) A “Virtuous IP Ecosystem” Is Essential to All Players in All Layers
As manifested in [Figure 4-7], an invention is transformed into a patent through a series of
processes, where different people with different types of expertise play their roles in each
of the upstream, midstream, and downstream elements of the IP ecosystem.

===== PDF_PAGE 143 =====
Chapter 4. Policy Recommendations
131
Some IP ecosystems can flourish, while others may perish. The main difference between
these two ecosystems usually lies in their efficiency or productivity in generating profits and
in distributing such profits to different players in different layers.
[Figure 4-8] Graphical Presentation of a Virtuous IP Ecosystem and a Vicious IP
Ecosystem

Source: Y. Shim (2016), “The IP Silk Road”.

[Figure 4-8] is a graphical representation of an IP ecosystem that flourishes in a virtuous
cycle or operates in a vicious cycle and perishes. As shown in the figure, the new
technologies and their patents are commercialized (or monetized) by various professionals
in the downstream of the IP ecosystem. When a technology commercialization succeeds,
the entrepreneur-inventor (of the 1st modality) can make profits from the sales revenue of
the product, which embodies the patented technology and which he makes and sells.
A licensor-inventor (of the 2nd modality) can also make profits from the royalty he receives
from the licensee-manufacturer. The plaintiff-inventor (of the 3rd modality) can make profits
from the damages which the jury or judge awards to the inventor. According to the incentive
theory of the patent system, as well as to our common sense, once an inventor gets his
share of the profits, the henceforth financially rewarded and emotionally empowered
inventor will go on to make the next generation of innovations, inventions, and patents. In

===== PDF_PAGE 144 =====
2022 K-Innovation Partnership Program with Azerbaijan
132
short, an IP ecosystem operating within the virtuous cycle always has channels through
which profits generated in the downstream can be funneled back to the upstream. As a
result, the inventors in the virtuous IP ecosystem are motivated to make inventions, to
obtain patent protection, and to proceed with the commercialization of their technologies
and patents. Because an IP ecosystem operating in such a virtuous cycle has a larger pool of
serious inventors, the patent attorneys or agents serving those inventors can also make
profits, as can the various professionals who assist them in commercializing their patents.
As this virtuous cycle goes on and on, the IP ecosystem flourishes while adding width and
depth thereto.
In an IP ecosystem which operates in the vicious cycle, however, everything goes in the
exactly opposite direction. For example, without knowing of the “vicious feature”, the
inventor invests his time, effort, and money in coming up with a decent invention. Then, the
inventor spends more money in filing a patent application and acquiring a patent. However,
the inventor cannot make any profits through commercialization due to, for example, a lack
of (or lax) patent protection. Although the persistent inventor may go through this vicious
cycle a few more times, it does not take long for the inventor to realize that making
inventions and innovations only help his copy cat competitors. The inventor w ill then
determine to join the pool of copiers and, as a result, there will be no one in the pool of
inventors and innovators.

(E) Invention-Rich Ecosystem Starts with a Pool of Innovative Inventors that Leads
to More Technology Transfer/Commercialization
Even within an IP ecosystem which operates in the virtuous cycle, not all inventions can be
commercialized. In fact, only a small fraction of the inventions can be transferred to a
manufacturer. In addition, only a small portion of such transferred inventions can be
commercialized. Furthermore, only a small portion of the commercialized inventions will
find a way to occupy a meaningful market share.

===== PDF_PAGE 145 =====
Chapter 4. Policy Recommendations
133
[Figure 4-9] Chances of Successful Technology Commercialization

Source: Y. Shim (2016), “The IP Silk Road”.

As mentioned above, at the global level less than 5% of the technologies developed in a
university “make it into an actual commercial product that generates revenue for both the
inventor and the university.”52
Assuming that successfully commercialized university technologies are those which are also
protected by patents, [Figure 4-9] shows a graphical representation of the chances of
successful technology commercialization, where the numbers of ideas, inventions, and
issued patents, each of which is required in order to reach the 5% level of successful
commercialization, are also shown.

This author further assumes in [Figure 4-9] that about 600 or more crude ideas would be
needed in order to generate about 300 patent eligible inventions. Even though there are no
statistical data to support this assumption, this 2:1 conversion ratio may even be too
optimistic, considering that most inventors are sincere and, therefore, serial and addicted
inventors, and that most inventors might even think up about 1,000 or more ideas before
coming out and filing 300 invention disclosures with the Technology Licensing Offices of
their universities. Whatever the actual conversion rates among crude ideas , inventions,
patent applications, and issued patents may be, the bottom line is that the most important
task of the government in promoting technology transfer and technology commercialization
is to build a pool full of lots (and lots) of inventors and innovators.

2.2.2 “Tech. Transfer” vs. “Tech. Commercialization”: Which One is
Easier to Accomplish? Which One is More Amenable to Quantitative
Analysis and Evaluation? Which One Better Suits the Government
Policy and Its Objective?
Although the terms “technology transfer” and “technology commercialization” tend to be
used interchangeably, they differ in terms of their definition and their scope. Therefore, one
needs to carefully compare these two terms before taking one of them as the objective of
the government policy.

2.2.2.1 “Short-Tailed” Technology Transfer vs. “Long-Tailed” Technology
Commercialization
Many treatises provide definitions of “technology transfer” and “technology
commercialization”, and such definitions may vary from one to another depending on the
source. For the purpose of comparison, this author consulted Wikipedia as the primary
source of such definitions. The following is the definition of the term “technology transfer”
given by Wikipedia:54

54 Noah Adam, PatentRebel (2019), ｢What percentage of patents are approved?｣.

===== PDF_PAGE 148 =====
2022 K-Innovation Partnership Program with Azerbaijan
136
Technology transfer, also called “transfer of technology”, is the process of transferring
(disseminating) technology from the person or organization that owns or holds it to another
person or organization, in an attempt to transform inventions and scientific outcomes into
new products and services that benefit society.55,56 Technology transfer is closely related to
(and may arguably be considered a subset of) knowledge transfer. A current and
comprehensive definition of technology transfer includes the notion of a collaborative
process, as it has become increasingly clear that global challenges can only be resolved
through the development of global solutions. Knowledge and technology transfer plays a
crucial role in connecting innovation stakeholders and moving inventions from creators to
public and private users.57
Alternatively, the following is the definition of the term “technology commercialization”
taken from Wikipedia:58
(Technology) commercialization is the process of introducing a new product or production
method into commerce, i.e. making it available on the market. The term often connotes entry
into the mass market (as opposed to entry into earlier niche markets) in particular, but it also
includes a move from the laboratory into (even limited) commerce. Many technologies begin
in a research and development laboratory or in an inventor's workshop and may not be
practical for commercial use in their infancy (as prototypes). The "development" segment of
the "research and development" spectrum requires time and money as systems are
engineered with a view to making a given product or method a paying commercial proposition.
The product launch of a new product is the final stage of new product development - at this
point advertising, sales promotion, and other marketing efforts encourage the commercial
adoption of the product or method. Beyond commercialization (in which technologies enter
the business world) may lie consumerization (in which they become consumer goods, as for
example when computers go from the laboratory to the enterprise and then to the home,

55 AUTM (2020), ｢AUTM 2020 Licensing Activity Survey｣.
56 Wikipedia (2022), ｢Technology transfer｣.
57 Narry Bozeman, Research Policy (2000), ｢Technology transfer and public policy: a review of research and theory｣.
58 Knowledge for Policy, Competence Center on Tech. Transfer (2022), ｢What is technology transfer?｣.

===== PDF_PAGE 149 =====
Chapter 4. Policy Recommendations
137
pocket, or body). (emphasis added).
As noted above, “technology transfer” refers to the transfer of the technology itself (along
with, or without, the patents which are related to the technology). Therefore, technology
transfer typically requires two parties, i.e. (1) the licensor-inventor or the owner of the
technology or patent who sells or licenses the patent to a third party, and (2) the 3rd party,
a licensee-manufacturer (or a licensee-service provider) that acquires the patent or obtains
the (exclusive or non-exclusive) license from the licensor-inventor.
It then follows that the scope of “technology transfer” includes the process of selling or
giving a license to the licensee -manufacturer, but does not include whether or not the
licensee-manufacturer actually completes the complex process of manufacturing,
distributing and selling the product, which itself embodies the acquired or licensed patented
technology. On the other hand, “technology commercialization” presupposes that (1) the
“technology” has been transferred and that (2) the licensee-manufacturer has embodied
the acquired or licensed technology into a product. In addition, “technology
commercialization” usually requires more participants than “technology transfer.” For
example, setting up a start-up would require a CEO and managers. Even when an inventor
sets up his own start-up, he should hire the managers anyway. In addition, when the
inventor desires to sell or license the patent, he would need an experienced patent broker
or intermediary. In other words, because “technology commercialization” encompasses a
broader scope than does “technology transfer,” it is not surprising that more people have
to be involved in the technology commercialization.
In this context, “technology transfer” may be viewed as having a short tail (in the relative
sense), while “technology commercialization” may be viewed as having a long tail (also in
the relative sense).
2.2.2.2 Is It Easier to Accomplish and Quantitatively Analyze Short -Tailed
“Technology Transfer”?
Due to the difference in the scopes of technology transfer and commercialization, it is safe
to assume that, other things being equal, accomplishing a certain target (e.g., in terms of a

===== PDF_PAGE 150 =====
2022 K-Innovation Partnership Program with Azerbaijan
138
number of technology transfer contracts signed) for technology transfer would be easier
than fulfilling the same target for technology commercialization. In addition, assessing the
efficacy of a certain government policy as well as conducting a quantitative analysis of that
policy would be easier for technology transfer than for technology commercialization. For
example, the quantitative assessment and analysis of tec hnology transfer is rather
straightforward, as all that one has to do is to find a number of signed contracts for assigning
or licensing university technologies or patents to manufacturers or other parties that have
paid money to the universities for such purchases or licenses. When desirable, one can
easily calculate the total amount of income made by universities within a given period.
In contrary the quantitative assessment and analysis of technology commercialization can
be rather complex. First of all, one has to establish the criteria for determining whether a
technology commercialization has succeeded or failed . Should a technology
commercialization be regarded as a success when a licensee-manufacturer (1) starts to sell
the products in the market ; (2) accomplishes a certain amount of sales revenue; (3) or
occupies a certain market share? Or should a technology commercialization be regarded as
a success if it (4) makes a net profit; (5) is acquired by a third party through an M&A deal;
(6) is listed in the stock market of a certain country; (7) or survives in the market for longer
than a certain period? and so forth. A government may establish and execute a policy
designed to spur and motivate the creation of innovations and inventions, and to promote
the transfer of university-born or research institute-born technologies and patents to the
private sector. However, when the government desires to analyze the outcome of such a
policy, it has to be careful in setting up the proper criteria for assessment and analysis of the
outcome.
At least in this respect, assessing and analyzing the outcome of a technology transfer would
be more amenable than assessing and analyzing the outcome of a technology
commercialization.
2.2.3 Korean Example: Korea Invention Promotion Association
KIPO (Korean Intellectual Property Office, formerly the Korean Industrial Property Office)
has been very active in nurturing the invention-rich IP ecosystem in Korea. Although many

===== PDF_PAGE 151 =====
Chapter 4. Policy Recommendations
139
commentators have criticized its quantity-oriented strategies, there is no doubt that KIPO
has been doing its share.

2.2.3.1 Brief History of KIPA (Korea Invention Promotion Association)
KIPO is affiliated with a dozen government-related institutions. KIPA (Korea Invention
Promotion Association), one of the earliest such institutions, was established in 1973. It has
contributed to developing the national economy and enhancing international
competitiveness through technological innovation in the industry by systematically and
efficiently promoting the invention promotion project and promoting the interests of
inventors. [Table 4-2] provides a brief summary of the history of KIPA.

2.2.3.2 KIPA’s Efforts to Establish an Invention-Rich Ecosystem to
Generate Lots (and Lots) of Inventions
In its 2022 announcement, KIPA stated the following four tasks as its key features and roles:
(1) Development of IP-based enterprises (support for the promotion of IP-based start-ups
and the creation of intellectual properties, etc.);
(2) Strategic use of IP (recovery and utilization of secured IPs, support for IP transactions,
etc.);
(3) Development of core IP human resources (inventive education promotion project, IP
human resource training project, etc.); and
(4) Dissemination and promotion of the IP culture (inventory day ceremony, development
and dissemination of international IP contents, etc.).60

Among KIPA’s abovementioned roles, the third and fourth are particularly worth analyzing,
as such projects aim to establi sh an invention-rich ecosystem in Korea (1) by nurturing
inventors of all ages and (2) by providing teaching and training on various levels of IP-related
matters to students, scientists, engineers , and laymen. To this end, KIPA has been
conducting diverse projects aimed at (1) establishing an invention -friendly intellectual
property ecosystem in Korea, (2) nurturing inventors of all ages and from all walks of life,
and (3) providing these inventors with the requisite IP education or training. The following
